<!-- lem-13: body_md -->
\label{lem_der_int} 
\label{lem_der_int:loc}
\label{lem_der_int:glo}
    Let $X : [0, T]\longrightarrow \mathbb{R}_+$ be a continuous function such that $X$ is differentiable, and let $1\le p<\infty$ be a Lebesgue index.
    
Assume that there exist a constant $B\ge 0$ and a measurable function $A :\mathbb{R}_+\longrightarrow [0, T] $  such that
\[ \frac{1}{p}\frac{d}{dt} X +BX\le AX^{1-\frac{1}{p}} \quad \text{a.e.} \quad \text{   on   }  [0, T].\]
Then, for all $t\in [0,T]$, we have
\[  X^{\frac{1}{p}}(t)+B\int^t_0 X^{\frac{1}{p}} \le X^{\frac{1}{p}}(0) + \int^t_0 A.\]
<!-- end lem-13 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Le $\varepsilon>0$.
It is easy to get the following inequality verified by $X$
\[ \frac{1}{p}\frac{d}{dt} (X+\varepsilon^p) +B (X+\varepsilon^p)\le A(X+\varepsilon^p)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}+B\varepsilon(X+\varepsilon^p)^{1-\frac{1}{p}}.\]

As $X(t)+\varepsilon^p>0$ for all $t\in [0,T]$, we have 
\begin{align*}
  \frac{d}{dt}\left(X+\varepsilon^p\right)^\frac{1}{p}+B \left(X+\varepsilon^p\right)^\frac{1}{p}\le A+B\varepsilon. 
\end{align*}
Then, integrating the previous inequality over $[0,t]$ yields for all $t\in[0,T]$:

\begin{align*}
    \left(X(t)+\varepsilon^p\right)^\frac{1}{p} +B\int^t_0 \left(X(\tau)+\varepsilon^p\right)^\frac{1}{p} d\tau\le \int^t_0 A(\tau)d\tau+t\varepsilon B.
\end{align*}

To obtain the desired inequality, it suffices to have $\varepsilon$ going to zero.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lem-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:A:posi:maxi}
         Let us consider $\mathcal{A}_\gamma\in \mathcal C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d\backslash \{0\};\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{C}))$,  homogeneous of degree $\gamma$, such that for all $\xi\neq 0$, $\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$ is diagonalizable, with positive real parts eigenvalues. Assume furthermore that  the matrices $\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$ is diagonalized by a matrix $\mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi)$,
         with the maps $\xi\mapsto \mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi)$ and $\xi\mapsto \mathcal{P}^{-1}_\gamma(\xi)$  bounded on any compact subset of $\mathbb{R}^d\backslash\{0\}$.

          Then, there exist two constants $c_0, C$ such that for all $p\in [1,\infty]$, $s\in \mathbb{R}$, $0<t\le T$ and $j\in \mathbb{Z},$ Inequality \eqref{maxi:regu:z} holds true.
<!-- end lem-14 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The Lemma \ref{lem:A:posi:maxi} is similar to \cite[Lem. A.2]{Danc22}). For the sake of completeness, we shall provide the proof. From Duhamel formula, 
   \begin{align}
       \label{sol:u}
     \Dot{\Delta}_j  u(t)= {\rm e}^{-t\mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)} \Dot{\Delta}_j u_0 +\int^t_0  {\rm e}^{-(t-\tau)\mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)} \Dot{\Delta}_j f(\tau)\cdotp
   \end{align}
   Hence it suffices to prove that there exists two positive constants $c_0$, C such that, for all $j \in \mathbb{Z}$
   \begin{align}
       \label{est:exp:p}
       \left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_j  {\rm e}^{-t\mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)}\right\Vert_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^d)} \dot{\Delta}_j z  \le {\rm e}^{-c_02^{\gamma j}t} \left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_j z\right\Vert_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^d)} .
   \end{align}

   
   Let us first prove inequality \eqref{est:exp:p} for $j=0$. Consider $\phi\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d\backslash \{0\})$ with compact support and value $1$ on a neighborhood of the support of $\varphi$ defined in \eqref{def:varphi:chap3}. We can write 
   \begin{align*}
       {\rm e^{-\lambda\mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)}} \dot{\Delta}_0 z &=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigl( \phi {\rm e}^{-\lambda \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\cdot)} \varphi \widehat{z}\bigr),\;\; \lambda\ge 0\\
       &= H_\lambda \star \Dot{\Delta}_0 z \quad \text{with } \quad H_\lambda\overset{\rm def}{=} (2\pi)^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} {\rm e}^{i x\cdot \xi }\phi(\xi) {\rm e}^{-\lambda\mathcal{A}(\xi)}d\xi.
   \end{align*}
   From Young inequality, it suffices to prove that 
   \begin{align}
       \label{est:conv:L1}
       \left\Vert 1\right\Vert_{L^{H_\lambda}(\mathbb{R}^d)}\le C {\rm e}^{-c_0 t}
   \end{align}
   to conclude that
   \begin{align*}
       \left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_0  {\rm e^{-t\mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)}} \dot{\Delta}_j z \right\Vert_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le  \left\Vert 1\right\Vert_{L^{H_\lambda}(\mathbb{R}^d)} \left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_0 z \right\Vert_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le C {\rm e}^{-c_0 t}\left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_0 z \right\Vert_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^d)},
   \end{align*}
  which corresponds to \eqref{est:exp:p} with $j=0$.

  The proof of \eqref{est:conv:L1} passes through the validity of the following inequality 
  \begin{align*}
      |H_\lambda(x)|\le C(1+|x|^2)^{-d} {\rm e}^{-c_0 \lambda}, \;\;\; x\in \mathbb{R}^d,\; \lambda>0.
  \end{align*}
   From integration by parts, one has
   \begin{align*}
       H_\lambda(x)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^d} (1+|x|^2)^{-d} h_\lambda(x), \quad \text{with} \quad h_\lambda\overset{\rm def}{=} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} {\rm e}^{i x\cdot \xi}\phi(\xi) ({\rm Id}-\Delta_\xi)^d \bigl( {\rm e}^{-\lambda \mathcal{A}_\lambda(\xi)} \bigr) d\xi 
   \end{align*}
   Recall that the differential of the exponential map may be written as:
   \begin{align*}
       D {\rm e}^X\cdot G =\int^1_0 {\rm e}^{(1-\tau)X}G   {\rm e}^{\tau X}, \quad \text{ } \quad G\in \mathcal{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R}).
   \end{align*}
   Hence, from the chain rule one may write
   \begin{align*}
       D_\xi\bigl( {\rm e}^{-\lambda \mathcal{A}_\lambda(\xi)}\bigr)\cdot G &=-\lambda\int^1_0 {\rm e}^{-\lambda(1-\tau)\mathcal{A}(\xi)}\bigl( D_\xi \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)\cdot G\bigr) {\rm e}^{-\lambda \tau \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)}d\tau\\
       & =-\lambda\int^1_0 \mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi) {\rm e}^{-\lambda(1-\tau)\mathcal{D}_\gamma(\xi)} \mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi)^{-1}\bigl( D_\xi \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)\cdot G\bigr)\mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi) {\rm e}^{-\lambda \tau \mathcal{D}_\gamma(\xi)}\mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi)^{-1}d\tau,
   \end{align*}
   with  $\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi) =\mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi) \mathcal{D}_\gamma(\xi) \mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi)^{-1} $ and $  \mathcal{D}_\gamma(\xi) $ is diagonal matrix with positive real part entries.
Remember that $\xi\in {\rm Supp}\phi$ which is a compact subset of $\mathbb{R}^d\backslash \{0\}$. Since $ \mathcal{P}_\gamma $ and $\mathcal{P}_\gamma^{-1}$ are bounded on any compact subset of $\mathbb{R}^d\backslash\{0\}$ and $\mathcal{A}_\gamma $ is smooth on $\mathbb{R}^d\backslash\{0\}$, there exists a constant $C>0$ such that
$$ |\mathcal{P}_\gamma^{-1}(\xi)|\le C,\;\;  |\mathcal{P}_\gamma(\xi)|\le C,\;\; |D_\xi \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)|\le C,\;\;\;\; \xi\in {\rm Supp}\; \phi. $$
Moreover, from \eqref{posi:cond:A} we deduce that there exists a positive constant $c_0$ such that the real parts of each entry of the matrix $ \mathcal{D}_\gamma(\xi) $ is bounded from below by $c_0$ on ${\rm Supp}\; \phi$. We deduce that (up to slight modification of $C$)
$$ |  D_\xi\bigl( {\rm e}^{-\lambda \mathcal{A}_\lambda(\xi)}\bigr)|\le C {\rm e}^{-\lambda c_0}. $$
By induction, one can get similar estimates for higher order derivatives of $\xi\mapsto \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$, which eventually yields
$$|h_\lambda(x)|\le C {\rm e}^{-\lambda c_0},$$
    and complete the proof of \eqref{est:exp:p} for $j=0$.
    
    The proof of \eqref{est:exp:p}  for any $j\in \mathbb{Z}$ can be deduced thanks to the homogeneity of $\mathcal{A}_\gamma$, and a suitable change of variables. In fact, setting $\zeta(x)=z(2^{-j}x)$, we have $\Dot{\Delta}_0 \zeta(2^j x)=\Dot{\Delta}_j z(x)$ and 
    $$ {\rm e}^{-2^{\gamma j}\lambda \mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)} \Dot{\Delta}_0 \zeta(2^j x)= {\rm e}^{-\lambda \mathcal{A}_\gamma(D)}\Dot{\Delta}_j z(x), \;\;\; \lambda\ge 0, $$
    which  reduces the proof of \eqref{est:exp:p} to the case $j=0$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lem-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:maxi:regula}
    Let us consider $S^0$ a positive symmetric matrix, $\mathcal{A}_\gamma\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d\backslash \{0\};\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{C}))$ Hermitian,  homogeneous of degree $\gamma$ such that the matrix $\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$                   
    satisfies \eqref{posi:cond:A}.
            
                   
        
                           
                 
                           
                                                                                                                   
               
                  
        
   Let $u\in C([0,T]; \mathcal{S}^{\prime})$ satisfy 
   \begin{align*}
       \begin{cases}
         & S^0 \partial_t u+\mathcal{A}_\gamma u=f\\
          & u_{|t=0}=u_0.
       \end{cases}
   \end{align*}
    Then, there exists two constants $c_0, C$ such that for all $p\in [1,\infty]$ and $s\in \mathbb{R}$, $u$ satisfies Inequality \eqref{maxi:regu:z}, for all $0<t\le T$ and $j\in \mathbb{Z}$.
<!-- end lem-15 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
According to Lemma \ref{lem:A:posi:maxi}, it suffices to prove that $(S^0)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$ is diagonalizable with positive eigenvalues.  Denote by $R$  the positive
symmetric square root of $(S^0)^{-1}$. Let  $\mathcal{A}_0$ be the
homogeneous of degree $0$ matrix such that 
\begin{align*}
    \mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi) = |\xi|^\gamma \mathcal{A}_0(\xi).
\end{align*}
 

As $\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$
 is Hermitian (so does $\mathcal{A}_0(\xi) $), then $ R\mathcal{A}_0(\xi)R$ is diagonalizable in an orthonormal basis, that is to say there exists a matrix $O(\xi)\in \mathcal{U}_n(\mathbb{R})$ such that $ H(\xi)\overset{\rm def}{=}   O(\xi) R\mathcal{A}_0(\xi)R ^*O(\xi)  $ is diagonal, with ${}^* O\overset{\rm def}{=} {}^T\overline{O}$. Moreover, taking advantage of \eqref{posi:cond:A} we discover that the entries of $H(\xi)$ are positive. Since  $ \mathcal{A}_0(\xi)= R^{-1} {}^*\!O(\xi)H(\xi) O(\xi)R^{-1},$ this implies that $(S^0)^{-1}\mathcal{A}_\gamma(\xi)$ is diagonalizablez by $O(\xi)R^{-1} $ with $|O(\xi)R^{-1}|\le |R^{-1}|$ for all $\xi \neq 0$.
 Then, using Lemma \ref{lem:A:posi:maxi} completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lem-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:equi:SK:positi:V1}
    Consider two $(n_1+ n_2)\times (n_1+ n_2)$ matrices A and B such that
    \begin{align}
        \label{form:A:B:appen}
        A=\begin{pmatrix}
            & 0 & A_{12}\\
            &A_{21}& A_{22}
        \end{pmatrix}
        \quad \text{and} \quad 
         B=\begin{pmatrix}
            & 0 & 0\\
            &0& B_{22}
        \end{pmatrix}
    \end{align}
    with  $A_{21}\in \mathcal{M}_{n_2\times n_1}$,  $ A_{12}\in \mathcal{M}_{n_1\times n_2}$ and $B_{22}, A_{22}\in \mathcal{M}_{n_2\times n_2}$. Suppose that
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $B_{22}$ is positive,
        \item $A$ is a Hermitian matrix.
    \end{itemize}
    Then, the following two properties are equivalent:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The matrix $A_{12}B^{-1}_{22} A_{21}$ is a $n_1\times n_1$ positive matrix.
        \item Condition (SK) holds true (see Lemma \ref{lem:SK_diss}).
    \end{enumerate}
    Moreover if we have  one of these two results, then $n_1\le n_2$.
<!-- end lem-16 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
First, assume that $A_{12}B^{-1}_{22} A_{21}$ is positive. Then, the rank of $A_{21}$ must be equal to $n_1,$ which implies that $n_1\le n_2$, and so does the rank of $B_{22}A_{21}$. Now, we observe
\begin{align*}
BA=
    \begin{pmatrix}
        & 0 &0\\
       & B_{22}A_{21} & B_{22}A_{22}
    \end{pmatrix}.
\end{align*}
Hence, the rank of 
$ \begin{pmatrix}
        & B\\
       & BA
    \end{pmatrix} $ 
is equal to $n_1+n_2$, and Condition (SK) is thus satisfied.


Conversely, assume that the matrix $ A_{12}B^{-1}_{22} A_{21}  $ is not positive definite. Since $ ^t \overline{A}_{12}=A_{21} $ and $B$ is positive, there exists $z\in \mathbb{C}^{n_1}\backslash\{ 0\}$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        B^{-1}_{22} A_{21} z\cdot  A_{21} z = A_{12}B^{-1}_{22} A_{21}z\cdot z=0.
    \end{align*}
    Then, under the assumption of positivity of $B$, we get $A_{21} z=0$. As we assume that $ A_{11}=0 $, then the vector $ ^t(z,0_{n_2})\in \mathbb{C}^{n_1+n_2} $ is both an eigenvector of $A$ and belongs to the null space of matrix $B$. Appealing to the $(SK)$ condition gives us a contradiction. This proves that the matrix $A_{12}B^{-1}_{22} A_{21}$ is positive.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lem-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:comp:lf}
    Let $s>0$ and $2\le p\le 4$. Denote $\frac{1}{p^*}\overset{def}{=}\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\cdotp$ Let $f$ be a smooth function on $\mathbb{R}$ with $f(0)=0$.
    Then there exists a constant C depending only on the threshold number $M$ between low and high frequencies and on $s, p, d$ and $f$, such that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:comp:lf}
        \left\Vert {f(u)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}^{l} \le C\left( \left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}^{l}+ (1+ \Vert u\Vert_{L^{p^*}})(\left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1}}^h +\left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}^{l})\right)\cdotp
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lem-17 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Set $  u_{M} \overset{def}{=} \Dot{S}_{M} u$. Next, decompose $ f(u)$ as follows:
\begin{equation}
    \label{decom:f(u)}
    f(u)=f(u_{M}) +\left(  f(u)-f(u_{M})\right)\cdotp
\end{equation}
   One the one hand,  according to Proposition \ref{propo:comp}, we have
   \begin{equation*}
       \left\Vert {f(u_{M})} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}} \lesssim \left\Vert {u_{M}} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}
   \end{equation*}
   from which we deduce that
                                                                                              
    \begin{equation}
    \label{ine:comp:low}
       \left\Vert {f(u_{M})} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}} \lesssim \left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}^{l}
   \end{equation}
On the other hand, according to \cite{HajDanChe11}[Lemma 262, p.94]  the following equality holds true in the tempered distributions
meaning

\begin{align}
    \label{decomp:fu:hf}
    f(u)-f(u_{M})= \sum_{k> M } f_k \quad \text{with} \quad f_j=f(\Dot{S}_{k+1} u)-f(\Dot{S}_{k} u).
\end{align}
Furthermore it holds
\begin{align}
    \label{def:mk}
    f_k= m_k\Dot{\Delta}_k u  \quad \text{with} \quad m_k =\int_0^1 f^{\prime}(\Dot{S}_{k+1}u+t\Dot{\Delta}_{k}u  )dt.
\end{align}
 Using the mean value theorem and the fact that for all $0\le t\le 1$,  $ \left\Vert \Dot{S}_{k+1}u+t\Dot{\Delta}_{k}u\right\Vert_{L^\infty}\lesssim \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty}$; $f$ and all its derivatives are bounded on $B(0,C\left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})$, we get   for all $q\in [1,\infty],t\in [0,1]$ 
\begin{align*}
    \left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{f^{\prime}(\Dot{S}_{k+1}u+t\Dot{\Delta}_{k}u)-f^{\prime}(0)}} &\lesssim C(f^{\prime\prime}, \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})\left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{\Dot{S}_{k+1}u+t\Dot{\Delta}_{k}u}}\\
    &\lesssim C(f^{\prime\prime}, \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty}) \left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{u}}.
\end{align*}
 As the operator $ \Dot{S}_{M+1}$ is continuous on $L^q$, we deduce 
\begin{align}
\label{est:f'(...):q}
    \left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{m_k}} 
    &\lesssim C(f^{\prime\prime}, \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})(1+ \left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{u}}).
\end{align}

 For all $j\le M+1 $, we have from \eqref{decomp:fu:hf} and the continuity of $ \Dot{\Delta}_j $
 \begin{align}
    2^{js} \left\Vert \Dot{\Delta}_j\left(  f(u)-f(u_{M}) \right)\right\Vert_{L^2} &\le \sum_{k> M}2^{-s(k-j)} 2^{ks}\left\Vert  f_k\right\Vert_{L^2}\nonumber\\
    & \le 2^{js} \left\Vert  f_{M+1}\right\Vert_{L^2}+\sum_{k> M+1}2^{-s(k-j)} 2^{ks}\left\Vert  f_k\right\Vert_{L^2}\nonumber\\
    &\le 2^{js} \left\Vert  f_{M+1}\right\Vert_{L^2}+ \sum_{k\ge j; \; k> M+1}2^{-s(k-j)} 2^{ks}\left\Vert  f_k\right\Vert_{L^2}
 \end{align}
where the last inequality comes from the fact that $ j\le M+1\le k$.\\
Observing that 
$$ \sum_{k\ge j; \; k> M}2^{-s(k-j)} 2^{ks}\left\Vert  f_k\right\Vert_{L^2} =(\alpha\star \beta)_j \quad \text{with} \quad \alpha_k= 1_{\mathbb{N}^*}(k) 2^{-k} \quad \text{and} \quad \beta_l = 1_{l> M} 2^{ls} \left\Vert  f_l\right\Vert_{L^2} $$ and using Young's inequality and then H\"{o}lder inequality, combined with \eqref{est:f'(...):q} with $q=p^*$, \eqref{def:mk} and \eqref{link:lf:hf}, we deduce that
\begin{equation}
    \label{est:lf:u:hf}
   \sum_{j\le M+1} 2^{js} \left\Vert \Dot{\Delta}_j\left(  f(u)-f(u_{M}) \right)\right\Vert_{L^2}\le  C(f^{\prime\prime}, \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})(1+ \Vert u\Vert_{L^{p^*}})(\left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1}}^h +\left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}^{l}).         
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lem-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:produ:lf:hf}
Assume that $d\geq2.$
Let $s\in\mathbb R.$ It holds:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item  If  $2\le  p\le 4$ and  $\sigma\geq d(\frac{p-2}{2p})$ then
\begin{equation}
    \label{lem:T:lf:hf}
    \left\Vert {\Dot{T}_a b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}}_{2,1}}\lesssim \left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}-\sigma}_{p,1}}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\sigma}_{p,1}}.
\end{equation}
\item  If   $2\le  p\le 4$ and  $s>-d/p$ then
\begin{equation}
    \label{lem:R:lf:hf}
     \left\Vert {\Dot{R}(a ,b)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}}_{2,1}}\lesssim \left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}-\sigma}_{p,1}}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\sigma}_{p,1}}. 
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
In particular if $ s> -\frac{d}{p}$,  $ 2\le  p\le 4 $ and  $\sigma\geq d(\frac{p-2}{2p})$
then we have
\begin{equation}
    \label{lem:pro:lf:hf}
    \left\Vert {ab} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}}_{2,1}}\lesssim \left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}-\sigma}_{p,1}}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\sigma}_{p,1}}{+\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}-\sigma}_{p,1}}\left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\sigma}_{p,1}}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-18 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Let us define the quantity  $p^*$ as follows: 
    \begin{equation}
        \label{def:p^*:T:R}
        p^*\overset{\rm def}{=} \frac{2p}{p-2}
    \end{equation}
    so that 
                                                                                                                          
    \begin{align}
        \label{ine:p:p*:}
         \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^*}=\frac12\cdotp
    \end{align}
We recall  the definition of the  paraproduct $\Dot{T}_a b$ of $b$ by $a$:
\begin{align*}
    \Dot{T}_a b =\sum_{k\in \mathbb{Z}} \Dot{S}_{k-1} a\Dot{\Delta}_k b 
\end{align*}
For all $k\in \mathbb{Z}$, as $\mathcal F(\Dot{S}_{k-1} a\Dot{\Delta}_k b)$ is  localized in 
the annulus $\bigl\{\frac1{12}\cdot 2^k\leq|\xi|\leq\frac{10}3\cdot2^k\bigr\},$  it holds
\begin{align*}
   \Dot{\Delta}_j \Dot{T}_a b =\sum_{|k-j|\le 4} \Dot{\Delta}_j(\Dot{S}_{k-1} a\Dot{\Delta}_k b ) \quad \text{for all} \quad j\in \mathbb{Z}.
\end{align*}
                              
    Using H\"{o}lder's inequalities and   \eqref{ine:p:p*:}, we have
    \begin{align*}
        \left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_j \Dot{T}_a b\right\Vert_{L^2}
                                                                                                                                        
        \lesssim \sum_{|k-j|\le 4 }\left\Vert p^*\right\Vert_{L^{\Dot{S}_{k-1} a}}\left\Vert p\right\Vert_{L^{\Dot{\Delta}_k b}}  .
    \end{align*}
Now, using  Bernstein's inequality, the definition of the low-frequency  cut-off operator $ \Dot{S}_{k-1}$  and the fact that $p^*\ge p,$ we can bound  $\Dot{S}_{k-1} a $ for all $k$ as follows:
\begin{align*}
    \Vert \Dot{S}_{k-1} a \Vert_{L^{p^{*}}}& \lesssim \sum_{i\le k-2}  2^{id(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p^*})}\left\Vert \Dot{\Delta}_i a\right\Vert_{L^p}\\
    & \lesssim 2^{k(\sigma-\frac{d}{p^*})} \left\Vert { a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}-\sigma}_{p,1}}\quad \text{if} \quad\sigma\ge \frac{d}{p^*}\cdotp
\end{align*} 
Then, using again \eqref{ine:p:p*:}, it holds
\begin{align*}
     \left\Vert  \Dot{\Delta}_j \Dot{T}_a b\right\Vert_{L^2}
                                                                                                                                                                               
     &\lesssim \sum_{|k-j|\le 4 } 2^{k(\frac{d}{p}+\sigma-\frac{d}{2})}\left\Vert p\right\Vert_{L^{\Dot{\Delta}_k b}}  \left\Vert { a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}-\sigma}_{p,1}}.
\end{align*}
We get \eqref{lem:T:lf:hf} by multiplying the last inequality by $2^{j(s-\frac{d}{p}+\frac{d}{2})}$ and summing over $j\in \mathbb{Z}$.
\smallbreak
Next,  as $1\le \frac{p}{2}\le 2$ and $s>-{d}/{p},$ we get \eqref{lem:R:lf:hf} as a consequence of  Bernstein's inequality,  Lemma 2.100 and Remark 2.102 of \cite{HajDanChe11}: 
\begin{equation*}
     \left\Vert {\Dot{R}(a,b)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\frac{d}{2}-\frac{d}{p}}_{2,1}} \lesssim \left\Vert {\Dot{R}(a,b)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\frac{d}{p}}_{\frac{p}{2},1}}\lesssim  \left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{-\sigma+\frac{d}{p}}_{p,1}} \left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s+\sigma}_{p,1}}.\end{equation*}
   Finally, putting \eqref{lem:T:lf:hf} (for the pairs $(a,b)$ and $(b,a)$)
   and   \eqref{lem:R:lf:hf} together yields \eqref{lem:pro:lf:hf}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- dfn-2: body_md -->
\label{def:Chemin lener}
    Let  ${a}$ in $[1,\infty]$ and time $T \in [0, \infty]$. We set
    \begin{align*}
        \left\Vert {z} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r})}\overset{\rm def}{=} \Vert 2^{js}\Vert \Dot{\Delta}_j z\Vert_{L^{a}_T(L^p)}\Vert_{l^r}\quad \text{with} \quad  \Vert z \Vert_{L^{a}_T(L^p)} \overset{\rm def}{=} \Vert z\Vert_{L^{a}(0,T;L^p)}.
    \end{align*}
    We then define the space $\widetilde{L}^{a}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r})$
 to be the set of tempered distributions $z$ over
$[0,T]\times \mathbb{R}^d$ such that $z \in \mathcal{S}^{\prime} $ and $ \left\Vert {z} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r})}<\infty.$
<!-- end dfn-2 -->
<!-- propo-4: body_md -->
Let $0<r<R$.
    \label{propo:Berstein:loc}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item   There exists a constant $C$ so that, for any $k\in \mathbb{N}$, pair $(q_1,q_2)$ in $[1,\infty]^2$ with $q_2\ge q_1\ge 1$ and function $u$ of $L^{q_1}$ with $\widehat{u}$ supported in the ball $B(0,\lambda R)$ of $\mathbb{R}^d$  for some $\lambda>0$, we have $D^k u\in L^{q_2}$ and
    \begin{align}
    \label{bern:ine}
        \left\Vert {q_2}\right\Vert_{L^{D^k u}(\mathbb{R}^d)}\le C^{k+1} \lambda^{k+d(\frac{1}{q_1}-\frac{1}{q_2})}\left\Vert {q_1}\right\Vert_{L^{u}(\mathbb{R}^d)}.
    \end{align}
    \item                                                                           
    For any  smooth function $M$ on $\mathbb{R}^d\backslash\{0\}$ with homogeneity $\gamma$, there exists a constant $C$ such that for any $\lambda > 0$ and any function $u$  with Fourier transform $\widehat{u}$ supported in annulus  $ \{ \xi \in \mathbb{R}^d \;\backslash \;r\lambda\le |\xi|\le R \lambda \}$ of $\mathbb{R}^d$, we have
     \begin{align}
     \label{bern:ine:M}
        \left\Vert {q_2}\right\Vert_{L^{M(D) u}(\mathbb{R}^d)}\le C^{k+1} \lambda^{\gamma+d(\frac{1}{q_1}-\frac{1}{q_2})}\left\Vert {q_1}\right\Vert_{L^{u}(\mathbb{R}^d)}.
    \end{align}
    \item There exists a constant $C$ so that for any $k\in \mathbb{N}$, $q\in [1,\infty]$ and function $u$ of $L^q$ with  ${\rm Supp }\;  \widehat{u}\subset \{ \xi \in \mathbb{R}^d \;\backslash \;r\lambda\le |\xi|\le R \lambda \}$ for some $\lambda>0$, W
we have 
    \begin{align*}
        \lambda^k \left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{u}(\mathbb{R}^d)}\le C^{k+1} \left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{D^k u}(\mathbb{R}^d)}.
    \end{align*}
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end propo-4 -->
<!-- propo-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:parpro:reminder}
    Let $1\le p\le \infty$ and $1\le {a},{a}_1,{a}_2\le \infty$ such that $1/{a}=1/{a}_1+1/{a}_2$.\\ For all $t\in \mathbb{R}$ and $s\le d/p$, we have
    \begin{align}
        \label{est:paraproduct}
        \left\Vert {  \Dot{T}_u v } \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s+t-d/p}_{p,1})}\le C \left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_1}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1})}\left\Vert {v} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_2}_T(\dot{B}^{t}_{p,1})}.
    \end{align}
    In general for all $s\in \mathbb{R}$, $t\ge 0$, we have
     \begin{align}
        \label{est:paraproduct:gen}
        \begin{split}
       & \left\Vert {  \Dot{T}_u v } \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1})}\le C \|u \|_{L^{{a}_1}_T(L^\infty)}  \left\Vert {v} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_2}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1})},\\  &\left\Vert {  \Dot{T}_u v } \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s-t}_{p,1})}\le C \left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_1}_T(\dot{B}^{-t}_{\infty,1})}\left\Vert {v} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_2}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1})}.
       \end{split}
    \end{align}
     If $p_1\in [1,\infty]$, $\varepsilon\ge 0$ and $s>-\min(d/p_1,d/p^{\prime})$, then
    \begin{align}
        \label{est:Reminder:gen}
        \left\Vert {  \Dot{R}(u ,v) } \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1})}\le C \left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_1}_T(\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p_1}-\varepsilon}_{p_1,\infty})}\left\Vert {v} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_2}_T(\dot{B}^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,1})}.
    \end{align}
    In particular for $s,t\in \mathbb{R}$ satisfying $s+t>d/p-\min(d/p,d/p^{\prime})$, we have
   \begin{align}
        \label{est:Reminder}
        \left\Vert {  \Dot{R}(u ,v) } \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}}_T(\dot{B}^{s+t-d/p}_{p,1})}\le C \left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_1}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,1})}\left\Vert {v} \right\Vert_{\widetilde{L}^{{a}_2}_T(\dot{B}^{t}_{p,1})}.
    \end{align}
<!-- end propo-5 -->
<!-- propo-6: body_md -->
\label{propo_commutator-BH:loc}
	 The following inequalities hold true:
   If $\sigma>0$
     \begin{align}
       \label{comm:est:a:b:sig>0}
        \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j] b\right\Vert_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le 2^{-q\sigma}Cc_j (\left\Vert \nabla a\right\Vert_{L^\infty} \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{2,1}^{\sigma-1} }+ \left\Vert b\right\Vert_{L^\infty} \lVert \nabla a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{2,1}^{\sigma-1} }  ) \quad \text{with} \quad \sum_j c_j =1.
   \end{align}
   
   In particular, if $\sigma\ge \frac{d}{2}+1$, then it holds
    \begin{align}
       \label{comm:est:a:b:sig>cd}
        \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j] b\right\Vert_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le 2^{-q\sigma}Cc_j  \left\Vert {\nabla  a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma-1}_{2,1}}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma-1}_{2,1}} \quad \text{with} \quad \sum_{\substack{j\ge -1}} c_j =1.
   \end{align}
	 Moreover if $-\frac d2 < \sigma \le \frac{d}{2}+1 $, 
      
                 
                        
                    
                                                           
                                                    
                  
               
   then
   \begin{align}
       \label{comm:est:a:b:loc}
        \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j] b\right\Vert_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le 2^{-q\sigma}Cc_j \lVert \nabla a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{2,\infty}^{\frac{d}{2}} \cap L^\infty}  \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{2,1}^{\sigma-1} }  \quad \text{with} \quad \sum_j c_j =1.
   \end{align}
                                                                                                                             
   We have also
   \begin{align}
       \label{comm:est:a:b:inf:loc}
        \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j] b\right\Vert_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le 2^{-q\sigma}C \Vert\nabla a\Vert_{\Dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,\infty}\cap L^\infty} \Vert b\Vert_{\Dot{B}^{\sigma-1}_{2,\infty}}\quad\hbox{for all }
        \ -d/2\le \sigma< d/2+1.
   \end{align}
 Finally If $\sigma>0$ and $k=1,\cdots, d$,
     \begin{align}
       \label{comm:est:less:gene:sig>0}
        \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j]\partial_k b\right\Vert_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le 2^{-q\sigma}Cc_j (\left\Vert \nabla a\right\Vert_{L^\infty} \lVert  b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{2,1}^{\sigma} }+ \left\Vert b\right\Vert_{L^\infty} \lVert \nabla a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{2,1}^{\sigma} }  ) \quad \text{with} \quad \sum_j c_j =1.
   \end{align}
  
   Similar results hold  true if we replace $L^2$ by $L^{a}_T(L^2)$ in the l.h.s and  the spaces $L^{a}_T(\Dot{B}^{s}_{2,r})$ (or $\widetilde{L}^{a}_T(\Dot{B}^{s}_{2,r})$) in the r.h.s.
<!-- end propo-6 -->
<!-- propo-7: body_md -->
\label{propo_commutator-BH}
	 Let $i \in [1,d]$ and $1\le p,\widetilde{p},q,r\le\infty $  . The following inequalities hold true for some nonnegative sequence $(c_j)_{j\in \mathbb{Z}}$ such that $\Vert (c_j)_{j\in \mathbb{Z}}\Vert_{l^r} \le 1$:
  \begin{enumerate}
      \item For all $\theta$ a $C^1$ function with $(1+|\cdot|)\widehat{\theta}\in L^1$
     \begin{align}
      \label{comm:est:1}
      \left\Vert [\theta(\lambda^{-1}D),a](b)\right\Vert_{L^p}\le C\lambda^{-1}\left\Vert r\right\Vert_{L^{\nabla a}}\left\Vert q\right\Vert_{L^{b}} \quad \text{with} \quad \frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{r}\cdotp
  \end{align} 
      \item  If $-\min(d/\widetilde{p}, d/p^{\prime}) < \sigma $, then
                                     
      \begin{align}
          \label{comm:est:p}
          \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j]\partial_i  b\right\Vert_{L^p} \le 2^{-j\sigma}Cc_j \left( \Vert \nabla a\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{\widetilde{p}}}_{\widetilde{p},\infty}\cap L^\infty}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r}}+ \left\Vert \nabla b\right\Vert_{L^\infty}\left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r}} \right),
      \end{align}
      and the second term is not needed if in addition $\sigma<\frac{d}{{\widetilde{p}}}+1$ or ($\sigma=\frac{d}{{\widetilde{p}}}+1$ and $r=1$).
    \smallbreak
    \item If $-\min(d/\widetilde{p}, d/p^{\prime}) \le  \sigma< \sigma<\frac{d}{{\widetilde{p}}}+1$,  then
    \begin{align}
    \label{comm:est:a:b:inf:glo}
        \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j] b\right\Vert_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)} \le C2^{-j\sigma} \Vert\nabla a\Vert_{\Dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,\infty}\cap L^\infty} \Vert b\Vert_{\Dot{B}^{\sigma-1}_{2,\infty}}\quad\hbox{if}
        \ -d/2\le \sigma< 1+d/2.
          \end{align}
        \smallbreak
\item     Finally, if $\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{\widetilde{p}}$ then
      \begin{equation}
          \label{comm:est:0}
          \left\Vert [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j]\partial_i  b\right\Vert_{L^p} \le 2^{-j\sigma}Cc_j \left(\Vert \nabla a\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{\widetilde{p}}}_{\widetilde{p},\infty}\cap L^\infty}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r}}+ \left\Vert {\nabla b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma-1}_{{\widetilde p},r}}\left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{q}+1}_{q,\infty}} \right)
          \end{equation}
          provided that $-\min(\frac{d}{\widetilde{p}},\frac{d}{p^{\prime}})<\sigma<\frac{d}{q}+1$ and  $r\ge 1,$ or $\sigma=\frac{d}{q}+1$ and $r=1.$
                    
                             
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
                  
 \end{enumerate}
<!-- end propo-7 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We invite the reader to consult Lemma 2.97 of \cite{HajDanChe11} for the proof of \eqref{comm:est:1}. The other two inequalities may be proved by taking advantage of Bony's decomposition. Indeed we have (with
the summation convention over repeated indices):
\begin{align*}
    [a,\Dot{\Delta}_j]\partial_i b=[\Dot{T}_{a},\Dot{\Delta}_j]\partial_i  b +\Dot{T}_{\partial_i \Dot{\Delta}_j b}a+\Dot{R}(a,\partial_i \Dot{\Delta}_j b)-\Dot{\Delta}_j\Dot{R}(a,\partial_i b)-\Dot{\Delta}_j \Dot{T}_{\partial_i b} a.
\end{align*}
According to Lemma 2.100 and Remark 2.102 of \cite{HajDanChe11}, the $L^p$ norm of each term of left-hand side (except the last one) is bounded by 
\begin{align}
    \label{ter:a:uses}
     2^{-j\sigma}Cc_j  \Vert \nabla a\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{\widetilde{p}}}_{\widetilde{p},\infty}\cap L^\infty}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r}}.
\end{align}

It remains to treat the term $ \Dot{\Delta}_j \Dot{T}_{\partial_i b} a$. To do this, one can simply use the fact that 
\[  \Dot{T}:L^\infty\times \dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r} \longrightarrow \dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r},\]  which gives 
the second term of \eqref{comm:est:p}.
This term is not needed if $-\min(d/\Tilde{p}, d/p^{\prime})<\sigma<\frac{d}{{\widetilde{p}}}+1$ or ($\sigma=\frac{d}{{\widetilde{p}}}+1$ and $r=1$),
since one can argue as in the proof of \cite[Lemma 2.100 and Remark 2.102]{HajDanChe11}.
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         

Finally, in the case $\sigma-\frac{d}{q}<1$ (or $ \sigma-\frac{d}{q}\le 1 $ and $r=1$), combining 

\[  \Dot{T}:\dot{B}^{\sigma-\frac{d}{q}-1}_{\widetilde{p},r}\times \dot{B}^{\frac{d}{q}+1}_{q,\infty} \longrightarrow \dot{B}^{\sigma}_{p,r} \quad \text{and} \quad \dot{B}^{\sigma-1}_{p,r} \hookrightarrow  \dot{B}^{\sigma-\frac{d}{q}-1}_{\widetilde{p},r} \]
with $\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{\widetilde{p}}$ for bounding 
$\Dot{\Delta}_j \Dot{T}_{\partial_i b} a$ yields \eqref{comm:est:0}.                                               
\\
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- propo-8: body_md -->
\label{propo_produc_BH}
     Let $(s,p,r)\in ]0,\infty[\times[1,\infty]^2$. Then $\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}\cap L^\infty$ is an algebra and we have 
	     \begin{equation}
	         \label{product_propo1}
	         \lVert ab \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}}
	         \le C(\left\Vert a\right\Vert_{L^\infty} \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}} + \left\Vert b\right\Vert_{L^\infty} \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}} ).
	     \end{equation}
	      Furthermore, if $-\min(d/p,d/p^{\prime}) < s \le d/p$, then the following inequality holds:
	      \begin{equation}
	         \label{product_propo2}
	          \lVert ab \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,1}^{s}}
	         \le C \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,1}^{\frac{d}{p}}} \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}},
	     \end{equation}
      and, if $-\min(d/p,d/p^{\prime}) \le s < d/p $ then, we have
       \begin{equation}
	         \label{product_propo:inf:p}
	          \lVert ab \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{s}}
	         \le C \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,1}^{\frac{d}{p}}} \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,\infty}^{s}}.
	     \end{equation}
	In the case $ s \ge d/p $,
                                                                                                              
    the following inequality also holds true:
      \begin{equation}
       \label{product_propo3}
        \lVert ab \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}}
	         \le C( \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,1}^{\frac{d}{p}}} \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}}+ \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,r}^{s}} \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p,1}^{\frac{d}{p}}} ).
	     \end{equation}
	  In the nonhomogeneous case, if $ s > d/p $ (or $s= d/p $ and $r=1$), 
	     \begin{equation}
	         \label{product_propo3:chap2}
	         \left\Vert {ab} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{p,r}} \le C\left\Vert {a} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\left\Vert {b} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{p,r}}.
	     \end{equation}
       Finally, if $(p_1,p_2)\in [1,\infty]^2$ with  $-\frac{d}{p_2}<s\le \frac{d}{p_1} \quad \text{and} \quad 2\le p_1\le p_2$, then, for $i=1,2$
      \begin{equation}
	          \label{product_propo:p1:p2}
	           \lVert ab \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p_i,r}^{s}}
	         \le C( \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p_2,1}^{\frac{d}{p_2}}}\ \lVert b \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p_i,r}^{s}}+  \lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p_i,r}^{\frac{d}{p_i}}}\lVert a \rVert_{\Dot{B}_{p_2,1}^{s-\frac{d}{p_i}+\frac{d}{p_2}}}.
	     \end{equation}
<!-- end propo-8 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
We provide the proof of these inequalities only in the homogeneous case. The proof of inequalities \eqref{product_propo1}, \eqref{product_propo2}, \eqref{product_propo:inf:p} and \eqref{product_propo3} is a direct consequence of paradifferential Calculus and Bony's decomposition 
         \begin{align}
             \label{bony:decom:app}
           ab=   \Dot{R}(a, b)+\Dot{T}_{b} a+\Dot{T}_{a} b\cdot
         \end{align}
         Hence, we will concentrate on the proof of Inequality \eqref{product_propo:p1:p2}. 
         Obviously, for  $i=2,$ it is just \eqref{product_propo3}
         with $p=p_2$ and $r=1.$
          Next, since $s+\frac{d}{p_2}>0,$ setting $1/\lambda=1/p_1+1/p_2$ then using 
          $$\Dot{R}:\dot{B}^{s}_{p_1,1}\times \dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p_2}}_{p_2,1} \longrightarrow \dot{B}^{s+\frac{d}{{p_2}}}_{\lambda,1} \quad \text{and} \quad \dot{B}^{s+\frac d{p_2}}_{\lambda,1} \hookrightarrow  \dot{B}^{s}_{p_1,1}$$ allows us to get the desired bound  for the first term of \eqref{bony:decom:app}.
          
          To bound the second term, since $s\le \frac{d}{p_1}$, it suffices to combine 
          $$\Dot{T}:\dot{B}^{s-\frac{d}{p_1}}_{\infty,1}\times \dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p_1}}_{p_1,1} \longrightarrow \dot{B}^{s}_{p_1,1} \quad \text{and} \quad \dot{B}^{s-\frac{d}{{p_1}}+\frac{d}{{p_2}}}_{p_2,1} \hookrightarrow  \dot{B}^{s-\frac{d}{{p_1}}}_{\infty,1}.$$ The last term of \eqref{bony:decom:app}  can be bounded in $\dot{B}^{s}_{p_1,1}$ with the help of 
          \begin{equation*}
          \Dot{T}:L^\infty\times  \dot{B}^{s}_{p_1,1}\longrightarrow \dot{B}^{s}_{p_1,1} \quad \text{and} \quad \dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p_2}}_{p_2,1}\hookrightarrow L^\infty.\qedhere
          \end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- rmq-7: body_md -->
We have similar results for the spaces  $L^{a}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r})$ and $\widetilde{L}^{a}_T(\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r})$ see \cite{Dan01loc,Haspot11}.
<!-- end rmq-7 -->
<!-- propo-9: body_md -->
\label{propo:comp}
     Let $f$ be a smooth function on $\mathbb{R}$ with $f(0)=0$. Let $(p,r)\in \times[1,\infty)\times [1,\infty) $  and
     $s\in\mathbb R$ satisfy:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{cond:on:p:r:s}
        s<\frac{d}{p}, \quad \text{or } \quad s=\frac{d}{p}\quad \text{and} \quad r=1.
    \end{equation}
    If in addition $s>0$ then, for any real function $u$ in $L^\infty \cap \Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r} $ we have $f\circ u$ in $L^\infty\cap \Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}$ and 
    \begin{align}
          \label{comp:u:propo:p}
        \lVert f\circ u \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}   \le C(f^{\prime},\left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})  \lVert u \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}.
      \end{align}
       More generally, 
      \begin{align}
          \label{comp:uv:propo:11}
           \lVert f\circ u- f\circ v \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}
           \le C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert (u,v)\right\Vert_{L^\infty}) (1+  \lVert( u,v) \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}}_{p,1}} )  \lVert u-v \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}} .
      \end{align}
   Next, under conditions $s>-\min(d/p,d/p^{\prime}) $ and \eqref{cond:on:p:r:s}, we have
      \begin{align}
          \label{comp:uv:propo:1}
         \lVert f\circ u- f\circ v \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}    \le C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert (u,v)\right\Vert_{L^\infty}) (1+  \lVert( u,v) \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}}_{p,1}} )  \lVert u-v \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}  .
      \end{align}
      Furthermore, if $-\min(d/p,d/p^{\prime}) \le s < \frac{d}{p}$ then the last  inequality remains valid for $r=\infty$:
         \begin{align}
          \label{comp:uv:propo:inf}
         \lVert f\circ u- f\circ v \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,\infty}}    \le C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert (u,v)\right\Vert_{L^\infty}) (1+  \lVert( u,v) \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{\frac{d}{p}}_{p,1}} )  \lVert u-v \rVert_{\Dot{B}^{s}_{p,\infty}}.
      \end{align}
       Similar results holds for  Chemin-Lerner spaces.
<!-- end propo-9 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
See \cite{DanADOlocal}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- propo-10: body_md -->
\label{propo_Compo_u_v_BH:loc}
  Let $0\le n_1\le n$ and $m$ be three integers. 	  Let $f: (X,Y)\in \mathbb{R}^{n_1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-n_1} \mapsto f(X,Y)\in\mathbb{R}^m $ be a smooth function on $\mathbb{R}^n$. Assume that $f$ is at most linear with respect to $ Y $ (that is, the second derivative $D^2_Y f$ of $f$ with respect to the component $ Y$ vanishes).\\
  If $f$  vanishes at $0_{\mathbb{R}^n}$, then for any $0<s\le \frac{d}{2} $  the following inequality holds true
  \begin{align}
      \left\Vert {f(u,v)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}\le   C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})( \left\Vert { v} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}(1+\left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})+\left\Vert { u} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}} ) . 
  \end{align}
  Furthermore if $-\frac{d}{2}< s\le \frac{d}{2},$ then we have
    for some  $C= C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert u_1,u_2\right\Vert_{L^\infty})$: 
    \begin{multline}
      \label{comp:u_v_1:2:BH:ine:prop:1:loc}
      \left\Vert {f(u_1,v_1)-f(u_2, v_2)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}\le  C  \left\Vert { v_2-v_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}(1+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})\\
      +C(1+\left\Vert {u_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})\left( \left\Vert {u_2-u_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}\left\Vert { v_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}+ \left\Vert { u_1-u_2} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,1}}\right)\cdotp
  \end{multline}
    Finally  if   $ -\frac{d}{2}\le  s< \frac{d}{2}$ then we have
  \begin{multline}
      \label{comp:u_v_1:2:BH:ine:prop:inf:loc}
      \left\Vert {f(u_1,v_1)-f(u_2, v_2)} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,\infty}}\le  C  \left\Vert { v_2-v_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,\infty}}(1+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})\\
      +C(1+\left\Vert {u_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}) \left(\left\Vert {u_2-u_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,\infty}}\left\Vert { v_1} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}+ \left\Vert { u_1-u_2} \right\Vert_{\dot{B}^{s}_{2,\infty}}\right),
      \end{multline}
      where $C= C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert u_1,u_2\right\Vert_{L^\infty}) $.
<!-- end propo-10 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
The Taylor formula and the fact  $  D^2_Y f=0   $ guarantee the existence of two smooth functions $\Lambda$ and $\Gamma$ defined on $\mathbb{R}^{n_1}$ such that
     \[    f(u,v) = \Lambda(u) v+ \Gamma(u) \quad \text{(with} \quad \Gamma(0_{\mathbb{R}^{n_1}})=0_{\mathbb{R}^m}\;\; \text{if}\;\; f(0_{\mathbb{R}^{n_1}},0_{\mathbb{R}^{n-n_1}})=0_{\mathbb{R}^{m}} .   \]

     
                                                                 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                   

     
If  $ 0<s\le \frac{d}{2}$ then, applying the inequalities \eqref{product_propo2} and \eqref{comp:u:propo:p}                                                                 
to the term $ \Lambda(u) v $ yields the first term of the right-hand side of  inequality \eqref{comp:u:propo:p}.
                                            
Next, using Proposition \ref{propo:comp} (recall that $s>0$) for the term $  \Lambda(u) $ gives the second term of \eqref{comp:u:propo:p}.                                        

 To prove \eqref{comp:u_v_1:2:BH:ine:prop:1:loc}  (resp.\eqref{comp:u_v_1:2:BH:ine:prop:inf:loc}), we use  the above decomposition to get
     \[  f(u_2,v_2)- f(u_1,v_1)= \Lambda(u_2)(v_2-v_1) +(\Lambda(u_2)-\Lambda(u_1))v_1 +(\Gamma(u_2)-\Gamma(u_1)).   \]
  Having this decomposition in hand, the first two terms of the last equality may be handled by  the  inequalities \eqref{product_propo2}  (resp. \eqref{product_propo:inf:p})  and  \eqref{comp:u:propo:p} (resp. \eqref{comp:uv:propo:inf}). Concerning the last one, we use Inequality \eqref{comp:uv:propo:1} (resp. \eqref{comp:uv:propo:inf}) .
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- propo-11: body_md -->
\label{propo_Compo_u_v_BH:glo}
  Let $0\le n_1\le n$ be two integers. 	     Let $f: (X,Y)\in \mathbb{R}^{n_1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-n_1} \mapsto f(X,Y) $ be a smooth function on $\mathbb{R}^n$ vanishing at $0$. Assume that $f$ is at most linear with respect to $ Y $ (that is, the second derivative $D^2_Y f$ of $f$ with respect to the component $ Y$ vanishes).
 
  Then, for any $0<s\le \frac{d}{2} $  the following inequalities hold true:
  \begin{align}
      \label{comp:u_v:BH:ine:prop:1:glo}
      \left\Vert {f(u,v)} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}}\le   C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert u\right\Vert_{L^\infty})( \left\Vert { v} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}}(1+\left\Vert {u} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})+\left\Vert { u} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}} ) . 
  \end{align}
  Furthermore if $-\frac{d}{2}< s\le \frac{d}{2}$ we have
     \begin{multline}
      \label{comp:u_v_1:2:BH:ine:prop:1:glo}
    \left\Vert {f(u_1,v_1)-f(u_2, v_2)} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}}\le  C  \left\Vert { v_2-v_1} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}}(1+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})\\
      +C(1+\left\Vert {u_1} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})\Bigl( \left\Vert {u_2-u_1} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}\left\Vert { v_1} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}}+ \left\Vert { u_1-u_2} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,1}}\Bigr)\cdotp
  \end{multline}
    Finally,  if   $ -\frac{d}{2}\le  s< \frac{d}{2}$ then we have
  \begin{multline}
       \label{comp:u_v_1:2:BH:ine:prop:inf:glo}
      \left\Vert {f(u_1,v_1)-f(u_2, v_2)} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,\infty}}\le  C  \left\Vert { v_2-v_1} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,\infty}}(1+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}})\\
      +C(1+\left\Vert {u_1} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}+\left\Vert {u_2} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}) \Bigl(\left\Vert {u_2-u_1} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,\infty}}\left\Vert { v_1} \right\Vert_{B^{\frac{d}{2}}_{2,1}}+ \left\Vert { u_1-u_2} \right\Vert_{B^{s}_{2,\infty}}\Bigr)\cdotp
         \end{multline}
  In the above two inequalities, $C= C(f^{\prime}, \left\Vert u_1,u_2\right\Vert_{L^\infty}) $.
<!-- end propo-11 -->
